### C3 (Direction review)

Topic: C3 — Tactile-Coupled Bimanual Cooperation. The bet: a *shared* inter-arm tactile-force channel beats per-arm fusion on holding-while-manipulating by ≥10 pp SR, and an explicit force-*balance* loss (lifted from two fingers to two arms) beats tactile-as-input on force-balanced handover.

#### Prompt 1 — Five Perspectives

**1. PRACTITIONER (builds/deploys daily).**
- *Core position*: The mechanism is plausible but the instrumentation cost dominates the science. To run H1 honestly you need two arms with synchronized, calibrated tactile at matched data budgets, the one variable academics under-price; in practice the "shared channel" win can be swamped by sensor drift, contact-mode chatter, and the latency of fusing two high-rate tactile streams into one representation.
- *Strongest supporting evidence*: [[2606.14218|UME]] reports 95% SR with real-time joint-torque feedback vs 0% for No-torque/UMI baselines on Box Flipping / GPU Picking, and 3.3× data-collection efficiency, which shows the gating factor is *getting force-observable data at all*, not the representation on top. [[2606.17055|T-Rex-Tactile]]'s +5% async-over-sync says sync is not free and not always the bottleneck. [[2510.14930|VT-Refine]] needed a custom piezoresistive sensor (FlexiTac) plus a GPU tactile sim just to get tactile to transfer.
- *Only-this-perspective tells you*: Pre-register the *matched-data, matched-latency* protocol before touching architecture; the most likely outcome is that "shared vs per-arm" ties once you control for sync quality, and the real publishable delta is the data-acquisition recipe, not the channel.

**2. ACADEMIC (knows the peer-reviewed literature).**
- *Core position*: The card's own table already shows the two sub-bets are partially answered on neighbouring axes: [[2605.13452|CUBic]] proves a *shared* tokenized channel beats per-arm coordination (51.8% vs DP 38.5%, shared-mapping ablation −9.5%) on *vision*, and [[2509.22421|Bimanual-Tactile-Reactive-MPC]] already learns an explicit cross-arm coupling matrix Cf with *shared tactile embeddings* (90–100% stable-grasp SR). So the live novelty is narrow: shared-vs-per-arm specifically on *force/tactile* for *holding-while-manipulating*, plus force-balance-as-objective for handover.
- *Strongest supporting evidence*: [[2602.13689|Symmetry-Aware-VT-Fusion]]'s bilateral force-symmetry loss is a *two-finger* result (96.59% insertion vs 93.23% vision-only), never lifted to two arms, which is exactly the H3 gap. [[2503.23271|Coordinated-Bimanual-State-Diffusion]] *names* cooperative force distribution as the objective but observes no force (reasons from vision/dynamics), so H1's "shared force beats reasoned force" has a clean baseline. SYMDEX (2505.05287, alphaxiv) does ambidextrous box-lift + handover with *morphological* symmetry but carries no force sensing, so it does not preempt the force claim.
- *Only-this-perspective tells you*: The contribution must be framed as a *controlled comparison* (shared force channel vs per-arm vs CUBic-style shared-but-vision vs Cf coupling), not a new SOTA system; a single shared-vs-per-arm ablation that ties collapses the whole bet.

**3. SKEPTIC (thinks it's wrong or already solved).**
- *Core position*: The "shared vs per-arm" distinction may be a distinction without a difference: a per-arm fusion policy with a coordination-capable backbone can *implicitly* reconstruct the transmitted coupling, which is precisely why [[2510.14930|VT-Refine]]'s coordination is "emergent" and still wins ~40% over vision. And classical dual-arm control solved internal/interaction-force regulation decades ago (projected inverse-dynamics Cartesian impedance, 1707.00484), so "force the arms transmit through the object" is not new physics, only a new wrapper.
- *Strongest supporting evidence*: [[2410.19235|DIPCOM]] gets 100%/95% peg insertion with per-arm F/T and *emergent* "continuous adjustment of the supporting arm", a direct counter to "per-arm cannot capture coupling". [[2404.16823|HATO]] does 10/10 Slippery Handover per-arm with no shared channel. The card's own falsifier admits the bet dies if per-arm ties shared *and* balance ties tactile-as-input, and nothing in the table rules that out.
- *Only-this-perspective tells you*: The biggest risk is not being scooped, it is being *null*: shared and per-arm tie at matched data, and the ≥10 pp claim never materializes because the backbone already absorbs the coupling.

**4. ECONOMIST (money/incentives).**
- *Core position*: Tactile-bimanual is where the data-moat money is right now (synchronized multi-modal episodes are expensive and proprietary), so the field is incentivized to publish *datasets and acquisition rigs*, not clean representation ablations. C3's bet is academically pure but commercially orthogonal to where the field is spending.
- *Strongest supporting evidence*: The card is saturated with data-substrate papers: [[2604.20444|VTouch++]] (120K episodes / 36M frames / 380 tasks), [[2606.17055|T-Rex-Tactile]] (100-hr dataset), [[2512.24653|RoboMIND-2.0]] (310K bimanual), [[2605.13083|TouchAnything]] (20-hr). The *method* deltas riding on them are small (T-Rex +5% async, RoboMIND "XR-1 gains"), evidence the marginal return is being chased on data, not architecture. [[2606.14218|UME]]'s headline is 3.3× *data efficiency*, a cost metric.
- *Only-this-perspective tells you*: A representation-only result will be cheap to ignore unless it ships with the data; the fundable wedge is "shared channel makes the expensive tactile data go further" (efficiency framing), not "shared channel is +10 pp".

**5. HISTORIAN (prior waves).**
- *Core position*: This is the end-to-end-vs-modular pendulum replayed in force control. Classical dual-arm manipulation explicitly decomposed motion vs *internal/squeeze force* (load-sharing, impedance, 1707.00484); the learning wave dissolved that into emergent per-arm policies; C3 proposes re-introducing the old internal-force variable as a *learned shared channel*. The same swing happened in tactile (model-based slip control → learned tactile policies) and in coordination (explicit cross-attention → CUBic's "let it emerge from a shared codebook").
- *Strongest supporting evidence*: [[2605.13452|CUBic]] explicitly frames the field's "decouple to avoid interference vs couple to coordinate" dilemma, the exact tension classical internal-force control named. [[2509.22421|Bimanual-Tactile-Reactive-MPC]] is literally classical MPC with a learned coupling matrix, a hybrid that sits where these pendulums cross. [[2602.13689|Symmetry-Aware-VT-Fusion]]'s "human motor control" symmetry prior is a re-import of an old bilateral-control idea.
- *Only-this-perspective tells you*: When the modular variable (internal force) is reintroduced, the winning paper is usually the one that shows the *learned* version recovers the classical guarantee at lower engineering cost, not the one that claims a raw SR bump; cite and beat 1707.00484-class internal-force control, or a reviewer will say you reinvented it.

#### Prompt 2 — Contradiction Map

1. **Direct clashes.**
   - *Skeptic vs Academic/Card on H1*: Skeptic says per-arm fusion implicitly captures the coupling ([[2410.19235|DIPCOM]] 100%/95%, [[2404.16823|HATO]] 10/10 handover, [[2510.14930|VT-Refine]] emergent +40%); the card/Academic say only an *explicit shared* channel optimizes the transmitted force. Concrete claim in conflict: does the ≥10 pp shared-over-per-arm gap exist at matched data, or is it ≤0?
   - *Practitioner vs Card on attribution*: Practitioner says any measured shared-vs-per-arm gap is confounded by sync quality/latency ([[2606.17055|T-Rex-Tactile]] +5% async-over-sync); the card treats the channel as the clean independent variable.
   - *Historian/Skeptic vs Card on novelty*: both say "force through the object" is classical (1707.00484); the card frames it as the live wrong assumption of the field.
2. **Strongest vs weakest.** Strongest = **Academic**: it is the most evidence-bound and its read (shared-but-vision already shown by CUBic; force-balance shown for two fingers; the only gap is shared-force-for-two-arms-on-holding) is directly supported by the card's own table and survives the alphaxiv check. Weakest = **Economist**: directionally right about data incentives but least able to adjudicate whether the *scientific* claim is true; it reframes rather than tests the bet.
3. **Resolving question.** At *matched data and matched tactile-sync quality*, does a single shared inter-arm force representation beat the best per-arm fusion (and tie-or-beat [[2509.22421|Bimanual-Tactile-Reactive-MPC]]'s explicit Cf coupling) by ≥10 pp on a holding-while-manipulating task? One ablation resolves Skeptic-vs-Academic-vs-card simultaneously.
4. **Universal agreement (likely true).** Tactile beats vision on contact-coupled bimanual ([[2510.14930|VT-Refine]] ~40% lift, [[2602.13689|Symmetry-Aware-VT-Fusion]], [[2505.18472|ManiFeel]] +26/+17 pp). Every perspective treats H2 as settled background, and the card itself demotes H2 to a "settled-background check". This is the safe floor; it is also why H2 cannot be the contribution.
5. **Blind spot (none addressed).** Deformable / non-rigid coupling. The whole "single shared transmitted force" first principle assumes a (near-)rigid grasped object where one internal-force variable is well-defined. For cloth, cables, soft objects, or sliding contact the "force each arm transmits to the other" is a *field*, not a scalar, and the shared-channel abstraction may break exactly where bimanual cooperation matters most. No perspective and no card row tests the rigid-vs-deformable boundary of the shared abstraction.

#### Prompt 3 — Synthesis Briefing

**CEO summary.** C3 bets that bimanual cooperation is governed by a single force transmitted between the arms through the held object, and that making that force an *explicit shared* representation (plus a two-arm force-*balance* loss) beats today's per-arm tactile fusion. The bet is real but narrow and partially fenced in: the field already shows a shared channel beats per-arm *on vision* ([[2605.13452|CUBic]]), an explicit force-balance loss works *for two fingers* ([[2602.13689|Symmetry-Aware-VT-Fusion]]), and an explicit learned cross-arm coupling already exists in classical-MPC form ([[2509.22421|Bimanual-Tactile-Reactive-MPC]]). What no one has done is the clean controlled comparison, shared-force vs per-arm-force vs explicit-coupling, on holding-while-manipulating, plus lifting force-balance from two fingers to two arms on handover. So the surviving novelty is a *mechanism-isolating ablation*, not a new system, and its central failure mode is a null result (per-arm ties shared once data and tactile-sync are matched).

**5 key findings, ranked by reliability.**
1. *Tactile > vision on contact-coupled bimanual is settled* (H2 is background, not contribution). Support: Practitioner, Academic, Skeptic, Historian; Economist neutral. Evidence: [[2510.14930|VT-Refine]], [[2602.13689|Symmetry-Aware-VT-Fusion]], [[2505.18472|ManiFeel]]. Challenged by: no one. Highest reliability.
2. *A shared channel beating per-arm is already demonstrated on vision* ([[2605.13452|CUBic]], shared-mapping ablation −9.5%), so the only open part of H1 is whether the shared quantity must be *force*. Support: Academic, Historian, Skeptic. Challenged by: Card (treats force-sharing as untested, which is technically true but narrow).
3. *Explicit cross-arm coupling with shared tactile embeddings already exists* ([[2509.22421|Bimanual-Tactile-Reactive-MPC]], 90–100% stable grasp), so a "single shared embedding" must beat this *intermediate* point, not just per-arm. Support: Academic, Skeptic, Historian. Challenged by: Card frames it as "strengthens H1" rather than threatening it; reliability medium-high.
4. *Per-arm/emergent fusion may already capture the coupling* (the null-result risk). Support: Skeptic (strong), Practitioner (via confound). Evidence: [[2410.19235|DIPCOM]] 100%/95%, [[2404.16823|HATO]] 10/10 handover, VT-Refine emergent. Challenged by: Card's first-principles argument; this is the central unresolved risk, medium reliability.
5. *The publishable value is more likely data-efficiency than raw SR* (shared channel makes expensive tactile data go further). Support: Economist, Practitioner. Evidence: [[2606.14218|UME]] 3.3× efficiency, dataset-heavy table. Challenged by: Academic (wants the clean ablation regardless); lowest reliability, most speculative but most strategically useful.

**Hidden cross-perspective connection.** The Historian's "reintroduce the classical internal-force variable" and the Skeptic's "the backbone already reconstructs the coupling" are the same claim viewed from opposite ends: classical control made internal force *explicit* because the controllers were weak; modern policies leave it *implicit* because the backbone is strong. C3 only wins if there is a *capacity regime* where the explicit shared variable still helps a strong backbone, exactly the regime where data is scarce (Economist) and contact is hard to instrument (Practitioner). The four non-card perspectives independently triangulate the same answer: the contribution is "explicit shared force as a *data-efficiency / low-data* inductive bias", not "explicit shared force as a ceiling-raiser".

**Actionable insight for the researcher (COMMIT / DE-RISK / DROP): DE-RISK, then commit narrowly.** Do not commit to the full "+10 pp" framing yet; commit to the *ablation* with a data-efficiency hedge. Concrete next experiment: on one holding-while-manipulating task with synchronized bimanual tactile (use [[2604.20444|VTouch++]] / [[2604.07335|TAMEn]]-class data), run a 4-way matched-data, matched-sync ablation: (a) per-arm fusion ([[2510.14930|VT-Refine]]/[[2604.07335|TAMEn]]-style), (b) explicit cross-arm coupling Cf ([[2509.22421|Bimanual-Tactile-Reactive-MPC]]), (c) single shared force embedding (the bet), (d) [[2605.13452|CUBic]]-style shared-but-vision as the force-agnostic control. Pre-register the matched-data/matched-latency protocol (Practitioner) and *sweep the data budget* so the headline can be "shared channel wins in the low-data regime" even if it ties at full data (Economist hedge). Add an explicit force-balance loss only on a handover task vs tactile-as-input (H3) once H1 shows signal. Kill criteria: if (c) ties (a) AND ties (b) across the data sweep, drop H1 and pivot to the H3 force-balance-for-handover result alone (lifting [[2602.13689|Symmetry-Aware-VT-Fusion]] to two arms), which is the cleaner standalone novelty.

**Frontier question.** Is "the force each arm transmits to the other" a single shared scalar/low-dim quantity at all, or does the right abstraction become a contact *field* the moment the held object is deformable or the contact slides, in which case the shared-channel bet is correct for rigid co-manipulation and wrong exactly where bimanual cooperation is hardest (cloth, cables, soft handover)? Answering this decides whether C3 is one paper or a whole sub-area.

---

#### Prompt 4 — Peer Review (independent)

*Reviewer stance: skeptical Stanford robotics faculty. Grounded against `Capability/Manipulation.md` lines 738-820 (C3: tactile-coupled bimanual cooperation; the bet is a shared inter-arm force channel beating per-arm fusion by ≥10 pp on holding-while-manipulating, plus an explicit two-arm force-balance loss on handover). The briefing under review is the CEO summary + 5 findings + hidden connection + actionable insight + frontier question.*

---

##### 1. CONFIDENCE SCORES (each 1-10 for reliability)

**Finding 1 — "Tactile > vision on contact-coupled bimanual is settled; H2 is background, not contribution." → 8/10.**
Correct and the card pre-concedes it (line 754: "Not 'bimanual is a vision-only problem' (refuted by VT-Refine), that contrast is now near-consensus"; H2 is explicitly demoted to a "settled-background check," line 793). The evidence is real and on-point: VT-Refine's ~40% real lift over vision-only on 2 mm-clearance assembly (line 764), ManiFeel's +26/+17 pp single-arm deltas (line 770). Two reasons it is not a 9-10. First, it is partly self-fulfilling: the briefing grades the card's own demotion as "settled," which is agreeing with the artifact, not independently testing the field. Second, and more substantively, the finding quietly conflates two different claims. "Tactile beats vision" (settled) is *not* the live assumption C3 challenges; the card is careful (line 754) that the live wrong assumption is "per-arm tactile fusion is enough." A reviewer should note the briefing's Finding 1 is true but is not the load-bearing background — the load-bearing, and far less settled, background is per-arm-sufficiency, which is Finding 4. Ranking the safe-but-irrelevant claim #1 and the dangerous-but-decisive claim #4 inverts the actual stakes.

**Finding 2 — "A shared channel beating per-arm is already shown on vision (CUBic, -9.5% shared-mapping ablation), so the only open part of H1 is whether the shared quantity must be force." → 5/10.**
This is the briefing's most-leveraged evidential move and it is softer than presented. Read the card's own gloss of CUBic (line 780): the architecture is "dual VQ codebooks with a shared mapping space, coordination emerges intrinsically." The −9.5% is an *ablation that removes the shared mapping from CUBic's own design* — it shows CUBic-without-its-shared-codebook degrades. That is **not** the controlled comparison C3 needs, which is *shared force channel* vs *per-arm tactile fusion as built by VT-Refine/TAMEn*. CUBic's "per-arm" counterfactual is its own ablated self on vision, not a competitive per-arm fusion baseline. So "shared beats per-arm is demonstrated on vision" over-reads an internal ablation into a head-to-head. The downstream inference ("the only open part is whether it must be force") then inherits that softness: if the vision result is really "CUBic's shared codebook helps CUBic," it transfers far less cleanly to "a shared *force* channel will help over a *tactile* per-arm fusion." Held at 5 because the direction of the evidence is right (shared structure plausibly helps) but the specific "already demonstrated" verb is doing more work than the −9.5% number licenses.

**Finding 3 — "Explicit cross-arm coupling with shared tactile embeddings already exists (Bimanual Tactile-Reactive MPC, 90-100%), so a single shared embedding must beat this intermediate point, not just per-arm." → 7/10.**
Well-grounded in the card (line 782): the paper learns a coupling matrix Cf for "how the velocity of one agent affects the hidden state of the other" plus shared tactile embeddings in a joint cost, 90-100% stable-grasp SR. The finding correctly raises the bar: the bet does not just have to beat per-arm, it has to beat an *existing explicit-coupling intermediate*. This is, in my judgment, the strongest genuine threat in the whole briefing and is arguably mis-ranked below Finding 2. One caveat keeps it at 7 rather than 8: the card itself flags (line 782) that this baseline is "classical-MPC-flavored (not an end-to-end policy)" and tests *stable grasp* (no-slip holding), not holding-*while-manipulating*. So "must beat this intermediate point" is correct in principle, but the 90-100% number is on an easier, narrower task (hold without slipping vs hold-and-do-work), and the comparison is policy-vs-MPC, which introduces an architecture confound the briefing does not flag. The threat is real; the specific number is not directly commensurable with C3's target task.

**Finding 4 — "Per-arm/emergent fusion may already capture the coupling (the null-result risk)." → 6/10.**
This is the correct identification of the actual crux, and the briefing deserves credit for naming its own central failure mode. The card's own falsifier (line 755) concedes the bet dies if per-arm ties shared. The supporting evidence is suggestive: DIPCOM 100%/95% with per-arm F/T and "emergent continuous adjustment of the supporting arm" (line 785), HATO 10/10 slippery handover per-arm with no shared channel (line 784). But "medium reliability" is right and I would not go higher, because the evidence is *off-task*: DIPCOM is peg insertion, HATO is slippery handover — neither is a head-to-head shared-vs-per-arm on holding-while-manipulating at matched data. They show per-arm *can be high* on *their* tasks; they do not show per-arm *ties shared* on *C3's* task, because that comparison has never been run (which is precisely the card's point). So the null-result risk is a well-motivated prior, not a demonstrated result. The briefing's phrasing "may already capture" is appropriately hedged; the 6 reflects "the risk is real and central, the evidence is indicative not dispositive."

**Finding 5 — "Publishable value is more likely data-efficiency than raw SR (shared channel makes expensive tactile data go further)." → 3/10.**
The strategic instinct is shrewd and I agree the fundable framing is efficiency, not +10 pp. But the *evidence cited does not support the mechanism claimed*, and a referee must flag this. UME's "3.3× data-collection efficiency" (line 778) is a *teleoperation throughput* metric: a torque-feedback exoskeleton rig collects usable demos 3.3× faster. That is a property of the *data-acquisition hardware*, not of a shared-force *representation* making a fixed dataset go further. The briefing (and analyst) silently slide from "force-observable data is cheaper to collect (UME)" to "a shared force *channel* is more data-efficient as an inductive bias" — these are different claims with different experiments (UME does not vary the representation). There is no paper in the C3 table that demonstrates a shared/coupled representation reaching matched SR at *fewer* tactile episodes than per-arm; that is exactly the experiment the actionable insight proposes to run, which means Finding 5 is a *hypothesis dressed as a finding*. The briefing even admits this is "lowest reliability," but the deeper problem is not low confidence, it is that the one number offered (3.3×) is the wrong number for the claim. 3/10: right strategy, mis-attributed evidence.

---

##### 2. WEAKEST LINK

**Finding 5 (data-efficiency), confidence 3/10 — and it is load-bearing for the whole "DE-RISK, commit narrowly" recommendation.** The entire actionable-insight pivot ("sweep the data budget so the headline can be shared-channel-wins-in-low-data even if it ties at full data") rests on the premise that a shared *representation* yields a sample-efficiency advantage. The only quantitative support offered, UME's 3.3×, measures teleop collection speed, not representational sample-efficiency, so the recommendation's escape hatch is currently unsupported. To verify, I need one of: (a) any ablation in the literature reporting matched-SR-at-fewer-tactile-episodes for a shared/coupled representation vs a per-arm one (none is cited and I doubt it exists, since the briefing proposes to be the first to run it); (b) a re-grounding of the efficiency claim on a representation-level number — e.g., does CUBic's shared codebook reach matched SR with fewer demos than DP/DP3, not just higher SR at full data? The card reports CUBic's SR (51.8 vs 38.5) but **no data-efficiency curve**, so even the vision analogue does not yet support the efficiency framing. Until a low-data crossover is shown for *any* shared representation (vision or force), Finding 5 is an aspiration, and the "commit narrowly to the efficiency hedge" advice is committing to an unmeasured quantity.

---

##### 3. BIAS CHECK — which perspective over-dominated

**The Skeptic over-dominated; the Academic was the secondary driver; the Practitioner and Economist were under-weighted relative to the load the briefing places on them.** Three of five findings (2, 3, 4) are Skeptic moves — "already shown on vision," "intermediate point already exists," "per-arm may already capture it" — and the CEO summary's verdict ("surviving novelty is a mechanism-isolating ablation, not a new system") is a pure Skeptic deflation. The Academic supplies the framing of the controlled comparison and the H3 standalone-novelty escape, but is mostly used to license the Skeptic's narrowing rather than to generate. The tell is Finding 5: it is *attributed* to the Economist and Practitioner, but the Economist's actual contribution (the field spends on data-moats, so a representation-only result is commercially orthogonal) is a sharper and better-supported claim than the one the briefing foregrounds (3.3× efficiency), and the briefing picked the weaker version. The Practitioner's genuinely decisive point — that any shared-vs-per-arm gap is *confounded by tactile-sync quality* (T-Rex's +5% async-over-sync, line 777, says sync is neither free nor always helpful) — is the single most important methodological constraint on the proposed experiment, yet it is compressed into one clause of the actionable insight ("matched-latency protocol") and never scored as a finding. A sync-quality confound that can fully explain a +10 pp or a null result deserved to be a top-3 finding; instead the briefing led with a settled-background claim (Finding 1). Net: Skeptic-heavy, Academic-as-enabler, Practitioner's confound buried, Economist's strongest version traded for its weakest.

---

##### 4. MISSING PERSPECTIVE — a 6th angle that would change the conclusions

**The Controls/Dynamics theorist (the person who has to define what "the force each arm transmits" even is).** Every perspective in the briefing argues about *whether* a shared force helps; none asks *whether the shared quantity is well-posed*, and that question changes the conclusion. For two arms rigidly grasping one rigid body, classical dual-arm control already gives the answer: the wrench space decomposes into a *motion-inducing* (external/manipulation) component and an *internal/squeeze* force that does no net work on the object, and only the internal component is the genuinely "shared, transmitted" quantity. A controls theorist would make three points the briefing misses. (i) The "single shared scalar/low-dim" framing is *under-specified*: the internal wrench of a two-contact rigid grasp is up to 6-dimensional, not a scalar, so "is it a scalar or a field" (the frontier question) has a known intermediate answer — it is a low-but-fixed-dim wrench for rigid grasps, becoming a field only for deformable/sliding contact. (ii) This reframes H1 from "does sharing help" to "does the *learned* shared embedding recover the *known* internal-force decomposition, and if so why not just compute it?" — which is the Historian's "cite-and-beat classical internal-force control" turned into a concrete baseline (project the measured per-arm wrenches onto the internal subspace and feed *that* as a hand-engineered shared channel). If that analytic shared channel ties the learned one, the contribution is null *and* the explanation is that the structure was computable all along. (iii) It sharpens the kill criterion: the briefing's 4-way ablation is missing a fifth arm — the *analytic internal-force* baseline — which is cheaper to add than any learned variant and is the strongest threat to novelty. **Net effect on conclusions:** this perspective would downgrade the "commit to the ablation" advice to "first establish that the learned shared channel beats the *closed-form* internal-force projection," because if it does not, C3 is not a representation paper at all, it is a re-derivation of grasp-matrix internal-force control with a neural wrapper — exactly the reinvention the Historian warned about, now made falsifiable.

---

##### 5. OVERALL GRADE + top fixes

**Grade: B.**

This is an honest, well-grounded briefing that correctly identifies the real situation — the bet is narrow, partially fenced by CUBic / Symmetry-Aware VT Fusion / Bimanual Tactile-Reactive MPC, and its dominant failure mode is a null result — and the "DE-RISK then commit narrowly" posture is the right call. The frontier question (rigid scalar vs deformable field) is genuinely good and identifies the real one-paper-vs-sub-area fork. It lands at B rather than B+/A- because three of its five findings have evidential or ranking problems that a referee would not let pass: the headline shared-beats-per-arm evidence (Finding 2) is an internal ablation over-read as a head-to-head; the data-efficiency finding (Finding 5) cites a teleop-throughput number for a representation-efficiency claim; and the two findings that actually decide the bet (per-arm sufficiency, and the buried sync-quality confound) are ranked below or beneath a settled-background claim. The synthesis is directionally trustworthy but its internal ranking does not track where the risk lives.

**Top fixes, in priority order:**
1. **Re-attribute or drop the 3.3× in Finding 5.** UME's number is teleop-collection speed, not representational sample-efficiency. Either replace it with a real data-efficiency curve (none exists in the table — say so) or demote the efficiency framing from "finding" to "the hypothesis the proposed data-budget sweep will test." The de-risk recommendation cannot lean on an unmeasured quantity.
2. **Add the analytic internal-force baseline as a fifth arm of the ablation.** A closed-form internal-wrench projection from the two per-arm F/T readings is cheap and is the strongest novelty threat (the Historian's "you reinvented classical control" made falsifiable). If the learned shared channel cannot beat the computed one, the bet is dead at far lower cost than the 4-way learned matrix.
3. **Fix the Finding-2 over-read.** State explicitly that CUBic's −9.5% is a *self-ablation* of its shared codebook on vision, not a shared-vs-competitive-per-arm head-to-head; "shared beats per-arm on vision" should become "shared structure helps CUBic on vision," which is weaker and changes how much it transfers to the force case.
4. **Promote the sync-quality confound to a top-line finding.** T-Rex's +5% async-over-sync means tactile synchronization can fully manufacture or erase a +10 pp gap; this is the Practitioner's decisive point and currently it is one clause in the actionable insight. It belongs in the ranked findings as a co-equal threat to Finding 4, because a positive H1 result that is really a sync artifact is the second null result hiding inside the first.
5. **Re-rank the findings to track stakes.** Lead with per-arm-sufficiency (the live wrong assumption the card actually challenges) and the explicit-coupling intermediate (the genuine prior art that the bet must beat), not the settled tactile-vs-vision background. As written, the ranking puts the safest-and-least-relevant claim first and the decisive risks fourth and fifth.

---

### D (Cluster review)

Topic: the coherence and shared-bottleneck thesis of Cluster D — "Dexterous & In-Hand Control" — whose Cluster-Overview row names the shared bottleneck as *"Multi-fingered contact is high-DoF, discontinuous, sim-to-real-fragile"* and whose intro paragraph frames the four directions as *"making control intent hand-agnostic (D1), bridging tactile sim-to-real without a tactile simulator (D2), unlocking emergent dexterity through exploration (D3), and bounding contact force with a hard constraint (D4)."* The question under review: does this grouping carve the dexterity problem at the right joint, and is each direction's shared bet sound enough to commit a publication to?

#### Prompt 1 — Five Perspectives

**1. PRACTITIONER** (builds/deploys dexterous policies daily)
- *Core position*: Three of the four bets terminate at a real-robot number that is nowhere near deployable, and the cluster quietly assumes an instrumented hand most labs do not own. D1's headline transfer is 40–60% zero-shot ([[2603.22264|UniDex]] 60%/40%, Wuji 40%), D3's flagship is 25% real peg insertion ([[2603.15789|OmniReset]]), and D2's anchor ([[2603.04531|PTLD]]) needs a privileged-sensor real cell — these are research floors, not products.
- *Strongest supporting evidence*: [[2505.14986|AnyBody]]'s damning result — multi-embodiment policies *match* single-embodiment on seen+interpolation but *collapse to 0%* on extrapolation. The "distinct hand" the whole D1 bet rides on is exactly the extrapolation regime where every measured cross-embodiment policy dies. [[2605.16257|DexJoCo]] shows the same fragility: DP-T 50.4% → 20.0% under randomization.
- *The one thing only this perspective says*: The cluster optimizes the wrong cost. D1 sells "5.2× cheaper than per-hand collection" ([[2603.22264|UniDex]]), but a practitioner with one hand (LEAP or Allegro) pays *zero* cross-hand premium and cares only about absolute real SR on *their* hand — for which a bespoke per-hand policy still wins. Cross-morphology is a vendor/fleet problem, not a single-lab problem; D1's economics only matter if you own ≥3 distinct hands.

**2. ACADEMIC** (knows the peer-reviewed literature)
- *Core position*: The cluster is intellectually honest in a way that quietly guts its own headlines — every direction's First-Principles row concedes the *parent* claim is already settled, leaving only a narrower within-axis head-to-head as the live novelty. The grouping is coherent (all four are post-grasp in-hand control phases under contact discontinuity), but "novel" has shrunk to "the controlled comparison nobody ran."
- *Strongest supporting evidence*: D2 states it verbatim — [[2405.07391|AnyRotate]] (CoRL'24, *two years before* PTLD) already did multi-axis reorientation with *no tactile sim*; D4 concedes [[2605.21811|Safe-Steerable-Geometric-Policy]] "states the soft-vs-hard dichotomy verbatim" and a survey ([[2512.11908|Contact-Rich-Safe-Learning-Survey]]) calls hard CBF-QP filters "a mature sub-field"; D1 admits [[2602.08278|DexFormer]] "states intent-transfer verbatim" and D3 dates its principle to [[1707.05300|Reverse-Curriculum-Generation]] (2017).
- *The one thing only this perspective says*: A reviewer will not credit "we ran the head-to-head" as a contribution unless the *outcome* overturns a belief. D1-H1 and D2-H1 are framed so that the *expected* result (intent transfers; pose-state is task-complete) merely confirms consensus — the paper is only interesting if the falsifier fires ([[2407.15002|GET]]-class graph matches intent; or contact-feature ties pose-state). Bet on the surprise, not the confirmation.

**3. SKEPTIC** (thinks the bet is wrong or already solved)
- *Core position*: D2's whitespace is the thinnest in the cluster and may already be pre-empted: if [[2405.07391|AnyRotate]] settled "no tactile sim," and [[2603.04531|PTLD]] already shipped the object-pose-state interface (+182% rotation), then the residual "interface head-to-head" is an ablation, not a paper. D1's "full reach→grasp→reorient→place cycle across distinct hands" is real whitespace — but only because it is *hard*, not because it is *unexplored*.
- *Strongest supporting evidence*: The D1 table is already crowded with 20+ cross-hand systems ([[2602.16712|Canonical-Hand]] 71% real zero-shot, [[2603.14522|One-Policy-Fits-All]] +19.8%, [[2603.10158|XL-VLA]] 0.72 mean SR, [[2511.01177|Scaling-Cross-Embodiment-World-Models]] novel 6/12-DoF hands) — each covers a *phase or sub-claim*, so the only unclaimed cell is "all phases + distinct hand simultaneously," which may be empirically unreachable given AnyBody's 0% extrapolation wall. The card's own falsifier ([[2407.15002|GET]] matching intent) is a live risk, not a strawman.
- *The one thing only this perspective says*: D2 and E1 are nearly the same paper. The card admits "D2 is the in-hand sibling of E1's Route-2 distillation." If the privileged-distillation mechanism is shared, the cluster may be double-counting one idea as two directions — and the genuinely novel three-way interface comparison (object-pose vs contact-feature vs tactile-sim) is one figure, not one project.

**4. ECONOMIST** (follows money/incentives)
- *Core position*: This cluster is shaped by who funds dexterous-hand fleets — the cross-morphology framing (D1) is a *vendor* narrative (one model amortized over a hand catalog) that academia adopts because it is benchmark-fundable, while the genuinely deployment-relevant bet (D4's fragile-force guarantee) is the least crowded *because* it has the smallest citation surface.
- *Strongest supporting evidence*: D1's anchors trace to FAIR/Meta ([[2512.13644|DexWM]], LeCun), NVIDIA/UW ([[2603.15789|OmniReset]]), and large-VLA labs ([[2602.19764|Multi-Sensory-Sparse-Experts]] vs RDT-1B) — players who *sell or scale* multi-embodiment stacks. The compute-cut metrics (42.6%, 5.2×) are the currency of industry papers, not lab papers. D4's hard-projection bet, by contrast, has *no* big-lab anchor and competes against a thin set ([[2510.25405|Stress-Guided-RL]], [[2509.18830|DexSkin]], [[2411.07833|DOBCBF-Grasping]]).
- *The one thing only this perspective says*: The benchmark substrate is forming *underneath* the bets and will dictate which direction is publishable. D2/D4 lean on benchmarks that barely exist ([[2001.03070|In-Hand-Manipulation-Benchmark]], [[2411.19408|SoGraB]], [[2605.27886|Tabero]] — all 2024–2026, none saturated). A direction whose head-to-head requires a benchmark *you* must build is a 2-paper bet (build the suite, then win on it), not 1 — budget accordingly.

**5. HISTORIAN** (has seen prior waves)
- *Core position*: D2 is the tactile re-run of the pixel-vs-latent world-model cycle — "simulate the sensor faithfully" (FEA/hydroelastic tactile sim) vs "skip the sensor, distill the privileged state" is the same modular-vs-end-to-end swing that played out in vision, and history says the *privileged-distillation* (teacher-student) side wins on sample efficiency but loses on the long tail. D1 is the dexterous replay of the cross-embodiment-transformer wave (Gato → RT-X → GET-Zero).
- *Strongest supporting evidence*: The teacher-student / privileged-distillation pattern D2 and D4 both rely on is the [[2210.13702|DeXtreme]] / OpenAI-Dactyl lineage (privileged sim teacher → real student), now a decade old; D3's reset-diversity lever is explicitly the [[1707.05300|Reverse-Curriculum-Generation]] (2017) principle, with [[2104.11203|Reset]] (22-DoF real) and [[2303.03486|SBRL]] as the high-DoF predecessors the anchor under-cites. Every "new" lever here has a direct ancestor.
- *The one thing only this perspective says*: The field has repeatedly mistaken *an instantiation at new scale* for *a new principle*, and D3 is doing it on purpose (it credits the lineage), while D1/D2 do it implicitly. The historical tell: when the anchor paper has to *name its own wall* ("performance saturation despite increased compute" — OmniReset), the contribution is usually "we pushed the existing lever harder," which ages into a footnote unless the emergent phenomenon (multi-phase at high DoF) proves to be qualitatively, not quantitatively, new.

#### Prompt 2 — Contradiction Map

1. **Direct clashes.**
   - *Practitioner vs Economist on D1's value*: Practitioner says cross-morphology is worthless to a single-hand lab (per-hand policy wins on absolute SR); Economist says it is the most *fundable* direction precisely because vendors/big labs need fleet amortization. Both can't be the right reason to commit.
   - *Skeptic vs Academic on D2*: Skeptic says the object-pose-vs-contact-feature head-to-head is "an ablation, not a paper" (PTLD + AnyRotate already exist); Academic says it is publishable *iff* the falsifier fires (contact-feature ties pose-state). The clash is whether a clean controlled comparison without a surprising outcome clears a top venue.
   - *Historian vs Academic on novelty type*: Historian says D3's "emergent multi-phase" is just the 2017 lever at new scale; the card (and Academic's charitable read) says emergence-at-high-DoF is a *new coverage phenomenon*. Quantitative-vs-qualitative novelty is the live disagreement.

2. **Strongest vs weakest.**
   - *Strongest*: ACADEMIC — it is the only perspective grounded in the cards' own concessions (every FP row admits the parent claim is settled), and it correctly reframes the bet as "win on the falsifier or don't publish." It survives contact with the actual text.
   - *Weakest*: HISTORIAN — its analogies (pixel-vs-latent, Gato lineage) are evocative but non-falsifiable; "this rhymes with a prior wave" does not tell the researcher which direction to commit to, and it risks dismissing genuinely new high-DoF emergence as "just scale" without evidence.

3. **The one question that resolves the biggest contradiction.** For D2 (Skeptic vs Academic): *On the same reorientation task under matched perturbations, does the object-pose-state interface ([[2603.04531|PTLD]]) beat the contact-feature interface ([[2405.07391|AnyRotate]]) by a margin large enough to flip a real design decision, or do they tie?* If they tie, D2 collapses to an ablation and should fold into E1; if pose-state wins decisively, D2 is a standalone paper. This single experiment also resolves whether D2 is a distinct direction or a duplicate of E1.

4. **What every perspective agrees on (likely true).** The four directions are a *coherent* partition of post-grasp in-hand control under contact discontinuity (no one disputes the grouping logic), AND in every direction the *parent* claim is already settled — so the surviving novelty in all four is a *narrow within-axis head-to-head*, and the real risk is not "is the theme right" but "is the residual comparison a full paper or a single figure." Universal across Practitioner, Academic, Skeptic, Economist, Historian.

5. **The blind spot (addressed by none).** *Inter-direction compounding error and the system question.* The cluster is presented as four parallel bets, but a deployed dexterous system runs them *in series* — D1's transferred control intent feeds D3's emergent behavior, which D2's estimator must perceive, which D4's filter must bound — and nobody analyzed whether the per-direction error rates (40–60% transfer × 25% emergent-real × lossy estimator × clamping SR cost) *multiply* into an unusable pipeline. The cross-references between directions are asserted (D4-H4 "wrap D3's emergent policy") but no perspective and no hypothesis measures the composed end-to-end success rate.

#### Prompt 3 — Synthesis Briefing

**CEO summary.** Cluster D is a genuinely well-bounded theme: four directions that cleanly partition post-grasp in-hand control (cross-hand intent, tactile sim-to-real, exploration-driven emergence, hard force-safety) under one real shared bottleneck — high-DoF, contact-discontinuous, sim-to-real-fragile multi-fingered control. Its intellectual honesty is also its strategic liability: every direction's own First-Principles row concedes the *headline* claim is already settled in the literature (no-tactile-sim by AnyRotate 2024; intent-transfer by DexFormer; hard-filter-beats-soft by Safe-Steerable-Geometric-Policy; reset-diversity by Reverse-Curriculum-Generation 2017), leaving the live novelty as a *narrow within-axis head-to-head* whose value depends entirely on whether the result overturns the expected answer. The cluster is the right place to work, but it is a portfolio of four ablation-shaped bets, and the researcher should commit to the *one* where the falsifier firing would be most surprising and most benchmark-backed — not spread thin across all four.

**5 key findings, ranked by reliability.**
1. *(Highest)* **The grouping is coherent but every direction's parent claim is conceded-settled, so novelty has collapsed to within-axis comparisons.** Grounded directly in the cards' FP rows. Supported by Academic, Skeptic, Historian; not challenged by anyone — Practitioner/Economist simply add orthogonal cost/incentive lenses.
2. **Cross-morphology transfer (D1) hits a measured extrapolation wall.** [[2505.14986|AnyBody]] = 0% on extrapolation; [[2605.16257|DexJoCo]] 50.4%→20.0% under randomization; transfer headlines are 40–60%. Supported by Practitioner, Skeptic; challenged by Economist (who says fundability, not deployability, is the point) and partially by Academic (the falsifier-firing case could still be a strong paper).
3. **D2 risks being a duplicate of E1 and an ablation rather than a paper.** Card admits "D2 is the in-hand sibling of E1's Route-2 distillation"; PTLD+AnyRotate already exist. Supported by Skeptic; challenged by Academic (publishable iff the interface comparison surprises) — this is the single most actionable disagreement.
4. **D4 is the least crowded and most deployment-relevant, precisely because it has the smallest citation/funding surface.** Thin competitor set ([[2510.25405|Stress-Guided-RL]], [[2509.18830|DexSkin]], [[2411.07833|DOBCBF-Grasping]], [[2503.07404|Safe-Robot-Foundation-Models]]); the open cell ("fragile gentle-force hard projection over a *learned* policy") is reinforced-as-open by every neighbor being model-based. Supported by Economist, Skeptic (genuine whitespace), Historian (privileged-distillation lineage); challenged by Practitioner (force tolerances are object-specific, limiting generality).
5. *(Lowest)* **The benchmark substrate the head-to-heads need barely exists, making several directions 2-paper bets (build suite, then win).** [[2001.03070|In-Hand-Manipulation-Benchmark]], [[2411.19408|SoGraB]], [[2605.27886|Tabero]] are all new and unsaturated. Supported by Economist; under-addressed by others — lower reliability because it is a forward-looking inference, not yet a measured fact.

**Hidden cross-perspective connection.** The Economist's "benchmark substrate is forming underneath the bets" and the blind-spot's "nobody measured the composed pipeline" are the same gap seen from two ends: there is *no benchmark that scores the full reach→grasp→reorient→place cycle end-to-end across distinct hands under a force ceiling*, which is exactly why D1–D4 must each be evaluated on a *partial-phase* proxy benchmark — and exactly why the cluster fragments into four ablation-shaped bets instead of one system paper. The missing benchmark is the thing that would let the cluster be a single strong contribution rather than four narrow ones.

**Actionable insight for the researcher — DE-RISK (do not commit blind, do not drop).** The cluster is worth working in, but committing to all four is a trap. Concretely: (a) **Commit to D1's H1 falsifier as the lead bet**, because it is the only direction where the *surprising* outcome ([[2407.15002|GET]]-class graph-joint-space matching explicit intent on the full distinct-hand cycle) would overturn an active consensus and the whitespace (full cycle × distinct hand) is real per alphaxiv (no single paper lands it). (b) **Immediately run the D2 resolving experiment** (object-pose-state vs contact-feature vs tactile-sim on [[2001.03070|In-Hand-Manipulation-Benchmark]], matched perturbations) as a *2-week probe* — if pose-state and contact-feature tie, fold D2 into E1 and reclaim the slot; if pose-state wins decisively, promote D2. (c) **Park D3 and D4 as second papers**, since D3 is conceded to be a 2017-lineage instantiation (commit only if emergent-multi-phase proves qualitatively new) and D4 requires building the per-step force-violation benchmark first. *Concrete next experiment*: on [[2505.14986|AnyBody]]'s extrapolation split, run intent-space ([[2603.22264|UniDex]] FAAS) vs implicit-history ([[2602.08278|DexFormer]]) vs graph-joint-space ([[2407.15002|GET]]) at matched data on the *full* reach→grasp→reorient→place cycle on one held-out distinct hand — the single experiment that either confirms D1's whitespace or kills it.

**Frontier question that would change everything.** Is dexterous *control intent* actually hand-agnostic, or does the right invariant live one level down — in contact *geometry/force* shared across hands — such that the entire cluster's "transfer the intent" framing (D1) and "distill the privileged state" framing (D2) are two faces of one deeper claim: *that the transferable substrate of dexterity is the object-contact interaction, not the hand and not the policy*? If yes, D1–D4 should be one paper about a contact-centric representation, not four about hands, sensors, exploration, and safety separately.

---

#### Prompt 4 — Peer Review (independent)

*Reviewer stance: skeptical Stanford robotics faculty. Grounded in Manipulation.md lines 821-1143 (Cluster D), read in full. The briefing is unusually honest, which makes it easy to review on substance rather than rhetoric. Most of my disagreements are with the briefing's own confidence calibration, not with its facts.*

---

##### 1. CONFIDENCE SCORES (1-10 reliability)

**Finding 1 — "Grouping coherent, but every parent claim is conceded-settled; novelty collapsed to within-axis comparisons." → 8/10.**
Directly verifiable in the cards and it checks out: D1 line 841 ("now consensus, DexFormer states it verbatim"), D2 line 915/921 (AnyRotate refuted it in 2024), D3 line 1001 ("not a fresh discovery"), D4 line 1081 ("states the soft-vs-hard dichotomy verbatim"). The finding is well-supported. I dock two points because "novelty has collapsed" is the briefing's framing, not the cards'. The cards argue the *parent* claim is settled but the *within-axis parameterization head-to-head* is live and unrun, which is a legitimate and common form of novelty (a controlled comparison nobody has run is publishable). Calling that a "collapse" overstates. A settled parent + an unrun decisive comparison is the normal shape of a good empirical paper, not a degenerate one.

**Finding 2 — "D1 hits a measured extrapolation wall (AnyBody 0%, DexJoCo 50.4→20.0%, headlines 40-60%)." → 8/10.**
Numbers are faithfully transcribed (lines 866, 865, 851/902). The wall is real and well-evidenced. I dock points because the briefing treats AnyBody's 0%-extrapolation as if it bounds D1's in-hand-hand-transfer claim, while the card itself (line 866) flags that AnyBody is *reach+push on arms/grippers, not the in-hand reach→grasp→reorient→place cycle on multi-fingered hands*. So the "wall" is measured on a different task family than D1's bet. It is suggestive, not dispositive, and the briefing slightly over-reads it as a hard ceiling for the specific claim.

**Finding 3 — "D2 risks being a duplicate of E1 and an ablation, not a paper." → 7/10.**
The duplication risk is admitted verbatim in the card (line 910: "D2 is the in-hand sibling of E1's Route-2 distillation"). The "ablation-not-a-paper" charge is a judgment, and the briefing correctly flags it as the single most actionable disagreement (publishable iff the three-way interface comparison surprises). I'd score this higher except the briefing under-weights its own evidence: the D2 table now carries a *five-point interface-lossiness ladder* (PTLD object-pose-state / AnyRotate contact-feature / Joint-Sensor proprioception-only 2605.21330 / DexNDM residual-dynamics 2510.08556 / variable-friction co-training 2503.02738). A clean controlled ladder across five no-tactile-sim routes is more than an ablation. The briefing's "duplicate of E1" worry and its "just an ablation" worry partly cancel: a five-rung interface ladder is exactly the kind of systematization E1 does *not* own.

**Finding 4 — "D4 is least crowded / most deployment-relevant because smallest citation surface; open cell is hard projection over a LEARNED policy." → 5/10.**
This is the weakest finding and the briefing's confidence here is miscalibrated. The claim that the learned-policy hard-projection cell is "reinforced-as-open by every neighbor being model-based" is contradicted by D4's own table. Line 1108: **Safe Robot Foundation Models (2503.07404) is explicitly "hard constraint-manifold action-projection over a LEARNED policy" with zero violations.** Line 1104: **ContactRL (2512.03707) is a "hard eCBF/QP shield over a learned RL policy," 0.20% violation + 87.17% SR.** So hard projection over a learned policy is *already demonstrated twice*; the genuinely open slot is narrower: hard projection over a learned policy *for a fragile gentle-force kPa/N ceiling specifically* (the cards say this correctly at lines 1108 and 1113, "REINFORCES the gap"). The briefing's CEO-level summary flattens "fragile-force ceiling over a learned policy is open" into "hard projection over a learned policy is open," which is false against the cited evidence. "Least crowded" is also doubtful: D4 carries ~18 rows including two benchmarks, a survey, and three distinct fragile-ceiling mechanisms. It is differently crowded, not less.

**Finding 5 — "Benchmark substrate barely exists; several directions are 2-paper bets." → 6/10.**
The forward-looking inference is reasonable and self-labeled lowest-reliability, which is honest. But the premise is overstated against the cards. D2 alone cites four benchmarks (In-Hand Manipulation 2001.03070, POMDAR 2604.09294, Sparsh 2410.24090, plus DexJoCo). D4 cites SoGraB 2411.19408 *and* Tabero 2605.27886, where Tabero explicitly already reports "per-step Max/Avg Transient/Applied force alongside SR at matched force conditions" (line 1072/1107). That is precisely the per-step force-violation-vs-SR protocol the briefing says must be *built*. So the "build the suite, then win" framing is too pessimistic for D4: Tabero is the suite, on parallel-jaw+GelSight. The real gap is narrower (no *multi-fingered, full-cycle, force-ceiling* suite), which is a legitimate but much smaller "2-paper" claim than stated.

**Mean: 6.8. Lowest: 5.**

---

##### 2. WEAKEST LINK

**Finding 4** (score 5) is the least-confident claim, specifically the assertion that D4's open cell — hard force-projection over a learned policy — is unoccupied and that "every neighbor is model-based."

What would verify (or kill) it: read the actual scope of **Safe Robot Foundation Models (2503.07404)** and **ContactRL (2512.03707)** against the fragile-gentle-force requirement. The card already concedes both enforce *generic* collision/kinematic/kinetic-energy constraints, not a kPa/N fragile ceiling. The verification step is one experiment-design question: can ATACOM (2503.07404) or the eCBF energy shield (2512.03707) express a per-finger force-magnitude upper bound as a differentiable constraint, or does the fragile-force ceiling require a genuinely new projection? If the former, D4's "open cell" is a re-parameterization of an existing method (a thin contribution); if the latter, D4 is novel. The briefing never resolves this, and it is the hinge of whether D4 is a paper or a tweak. Until that is checked, treating D4 as "the least crowded, most deployment-relevant" lead candidate is unjustified.

---

##### 3. BIAS CHECK — which perspective over-dominated

**Skeptic-dominant, with the Historian as enabler.** The Skeptic appears in the support set of all five findings; the Historian's "this principle is from 2017 / CoRL'24" framing supplies the recurring "conceded-settled" verdict that drives the headline. The synthesis is structurally a settledness-audit: every finding reduces to "the parent claim is old, so what's left is a comparison." That lens is valuable but it systematically under-weights the Academic's counter-position — that a clean, decisive, never-run controlled comparison *is* a contribution (NeurIPS/CoRL/RSS routinely accept "we ran the head-to-head everyone assumed the answer to and the answer flipped"). The Practitioner is present but thin (one object-specificity caveat in finding 4). The Economist's contribution (benchmark substrate, fundability-not-deployability) is genuine and orthogonal but appears mostly as a frame, not as a constraint that changes a recommendation. Net: the briefing is tuned to find reasons a direction is *derivative*, and is less equipped to recognize when a derivative-looking direction is the right empirical bet. That is a Skeptic bias, not balance.

---

##### 4. MISSING PERSPECTIVE — the 6th angle

**The Experimentalist / Apparatus-cost realist.** Every one of the briefing's recommended experiments requires hardware that is asymmetrically expensive and is never priced. D1's lead experiment (intent vs implicit-history vs graph-joint-space on the full reach→grasp→reorient→place cycle on a held-out *distinct physical hand*) needs at least two physically different multi-fingered hands instrumented identically, plus matched-data collection on each: this is a multi-month, multi-hand rig, not a 2-week probe. D2's "2-week probe" needs an instrumented privileged-pose real cell (the card's own Risk row, line 982, flags this as a one-time but real cost). D4 needs fragile-object integrity measurement apparatus (DACD scanning, SoGraB-style). An apparatus-cost lens reorders the portfolio: the briefing recommends D1 as lead and D2 as a fast probe, but on a feasibility-adjusted basis **D2 is the cheapest decisive experiment** (one hand, one instrumented cell, simulator-side interface swap) and **D1 is the most expensive** (the distinct-physical-hand requirement is the entire bet and also the entire cost). A reviewer funding one student would invert the briefing's lead recommendation: run D2's interface ladder first because it is the only bet whose falsifier can fire on existing hardware in a quarter, and treat D1's full-cycle distinct-hand claim as the expensive second paper. This sixth lens changes the actionable conclusion, which is exactly the test for a missing perspective.

---

##### 5. OVERALL GRADE + top fixes

**Grade: B+.**

This is a strong, intellectually honest briefing. It correctly identifies a real shared bottleneck, it does not hide that the parent claims are settled, and its frontier question (is the invariant the hand, or one level down at object-contact interaction?) is genuinely the right question and arguably the best sentence in the document. What keeps it from an A-range grade is calibration: the synthesis's confidence is highest exactly where the cards contradict it (D4) and its lead recommendation ignores the apparatus-cost reality that should reorder the portfolio. A Stanford committee would accept the analysis and reject the prioritization.

**Top fixes, in order:**
1. **Correct the D4 over-reach (top fix).** The briefing's finding 4 claims hard projection over a learned policy is open; the cards cite two papers (2503.07404, 2512.03707) that already do it. Rewrite the open cell to its true, narrower scope — *fragile gentle-force kPa/N ceiling over a learned policy* — and demote D4 from "most deployment-relevant lead-adjacent" to "second paper, contingent on confirming ATACOM/eCBF cannot already express the force-magnitude bound." This is a factual correction, not a taste call.
2. **Re-price the portfolio by apparatus cost and likely invert the lead.** Make D2 (cheapest decisive falsifier, one hand) the immediate bet and D1 (most expensive, distinct-physical-hand) the funded second paper. The briefing's current order optimizes for "which falsifier would surprise most," ignoring "which falsifier can fire at all this quarter."
3. **Stop calling a never-run controlled comparison a 'collapse of novelty.'** Reframe findings 1 and 3 to acknowledge the Academic's point: a clean, decisive, surprising head-to-head is a contribution. The briefing's own D2 five-rung interface ladder and D4 Tabero-already-exists evidence show several of these comparisons are richer than "ablation."
4. **Tighten the benchmark-substrate claim (finding 5).** Tabero already supplies the per-step-force-vs-SR protocol; the real gap is the *multi-fingered full-cycle force-ceiling* suite, not benchmarks in general. Say the smaller true thing.
