### E2 (Direction review)

Topic: the card's bet — "Under a leave-one-sensor-out (N−1) protocol where the held-out sensor is unseen *by the encoder*, deployable-policy-SR retention rises with training-sensor *diversity* and clears ≥80% by a small N — beyond UniForce's 90–120% (which saw all 3 sensors) and TaF-VLA's 60.3% family-level ceiling. Falsifiable: if held-out-sensor policy-SR retention plateaus below 80% regardless of how many sensors the encoder trains on, the ceiling is fundamental (a visual-to-tactile floor), not data-limited."

#### Prompt 1 — Five Perspectives

**1. PRACTITIONER (builds/deploys tactile policies daily).**
- *Core position*: The N−1 sweep is methodologically clean but operationally a luxury — a deploying lab owns 1-2 sensor types and re-calibrates per-fingertip after every gel swap, so "retention vs sensor *count*" is a quantity nobody on a real rig can act on until N is large, and N is exactly what's scarce. The load-bearing reality the card under-weights: the held-out *sensor* is never held constant — gel wear, illumination drift, and mounting reseat move a GelSight's distribution within one afternoon, so a frozen "leave-one-sensor-out" split overstates portability.
- *Strongest supporting evidence*: ManiFeel ([[2505.18472]]) — the one harness the card leans on — itself "finds existing models lack universal generalization," and it is a *single simulated sensor*; roto 2.0 ([[2605.21429]]) varies hand *morphology*, not sensor type. So the card's two "standardized policy-SR substrates" each measure something adjacent to the bet, not the bet. UniForce's headline ([[2602.01153]]) is wiping-SR 20%→80% on *one* task with *bilateral* tactile (single-sensor only 50-60%), a thin policy-SR base to build a scaling law on.
- *The one thing only this perspective says*: Before running an N-sweep, instrument *within-sensor* distribution drift (gel wear, reseat) and report it alongside cross-sensor retention — otherwise the "fundamental floor vs data-limited" dichotomy is confounded by nuisance variance you never controlled, and reviewers who deploy will know it.

**2. ACADEMIC (knows the peer-reviewed literature).**
- *Core position*: The bet is a well-posed, genuinely unrun measurement — no cited paper runs a strict *encoder-held-out* sweep over sensor *count* against deployable policy-SR — but the framing oversells "invariance is settled." UniForce's force-grounding is r=−0.74 / R²≈0.55 on z5↔Fz: that is a *correlation*, explaining ~55% of normal-force variance on one latent dim, which is evidence of partial grounding, not a proof that invariance is "by construction."
- *Strongest supporting evidence*: The card's own table shows the metric-substitution problem the bet is built on is real: SITR ([[2502.19638]]) reports 81.94% inter-sensor *classification*, T3 ([[2406.13640]]) reports +24% classification / +25% insertion-SR — all perception or single-task SR, none a retention-vs-diversity curve. FTP-1 ([[2606.13102]], confirmed via full content) tests exactly *two* unseen sensors (Xense, Contactile) at *fixed* diversity (21 training sensors), +31.6% over no-pretrain — the only cross-sensor held-out *policy*-SR number in the literature, but a single point, not a curve.
- *The one thing only this perspective says*: H2 (policy-SR vs classification accuracy decoupling) is the *strongest and most publishable* sub-claim — it is a clean negative result the field needs — and it does not depend on the scaling law being monotone. Lead with H2, not H1.

**3. SKEPTIC (thinks the bet is wrong or already mostly answered).**
- *Core position*: The card already concedes the bet to within a hair: UniForce 90–120% retention "(but saw all 3 sensors)" and FTP-1 +31.6% on unseen sensors mean cross-sensor deployable transfer *works today*; the only thing left is the *shape of a curve*, and a curve is not a result if it comes out boring (monotone-and-saturating, which is what every scaling-law paper finds). The "fundamental visual-to-tactile floor" falsifier is a heads-I-win-tails-I-still-publish framing — but a plateau at, say, 78% is scientifically indistinguishable from "data-limited but we didn't have enough sensors," so the falsifier may be unfalsifiable in practice with the 3-4 sensors actually available.
- *Strongest supporting evidence*: The conveniently-ignored fact — there are only ~3-5 *physically distinct* sensing principles (optical/vision-based, magnetic, capacitive, piezoresistive, array). UniForce already spans vision-based + magnetic; TaF-VLA ([[2601.20321]]) spans VBTS families at 60.3%. An "N-sweep" over 3-4 points is a line through near-saturation, not a law. CTTP ([[2410.11834]]) already gets cross-sensor zero-shot peg-insertion 18 vs 5 on a *two*-sensor pair — strong transfer with N=1 source.
- *The one thing only this perspective says*: State the minimum N at which the curve becomes informative *before* running it. If 4 sensors is the realistic ceiling and the field already shows transfer at N=1-2, the scaling claim has ~2-3 points of headroom — pre-register the number of sensors and the effect size that would count as "monotone diversity gain," or the result will read as a sweep of a saturated quantity.

**4. ECONOMIST (follows money/incentives).**
- *Core position*: This bet sits in a benchmark-creation niche, which is the highest-leverage academic move when a field is pre-standardization — own the protocol (the "N−1 encoder-held-out policy-SR retention curve") and you collect citations from everyone who later runs it. But the incentive that *shaped* the card is benchmark-chasing: roto 2.0, ManiFeel, HT-Bench ([[2606.19161]]) all landed in months, so the substrate is a moving target and a late protocol gets absorbed into the next omnibus benchmark rather than cited as the origin.
- *Strongest supporting evidence*: The card lists 4 benchmarks in the anchor row alone (roto 2.0, HT-Bench, ManiFeel, plus Hoi! as held-out ref) and ~28 related papers — a field minting one tactile-foundation artifact per month (Sparsh-X, AnyTouch 2 [[2602.09617]], FTP-1, UniForce all <12 months). Hardware vendors (Sharpa behind FTP-1, GelSight/DIGIT ecosystem) profit from a "portability" narrative because it sells *more* sensor SKUs, not fewer — the invariance story is commercially convenient regardless of whether it scales.
- *The one thing only this perspective says*: The defensible asset here is *the protocol + a public N−1 leaderboard*, not the policy. If you commit, ship the harness and split as the contribution (a "leave-one-sensor-out policy-SR" track on roto 2.0 / ManiFeel) within one cycle, before HT-Bench's successor swallows it; the scaling-law plot is the demo, the protocol is the moat.

**5. HISTORIAN (has seen prior waves).**
- *Core position*: This is the tactile re-run of the 2023-25 *cross-embodiment* wave (RT-X / Open-X-Embodiment, Octo, embodiment scaling laws): a heterogeneous-data foundation model, a "does diversity buy generalization?" question, and a held-out-embodiment sweep. That wave's verdict is the prior here — diversity helped but *sub-linearly and with sharp saturation*, and the headline papers were the *datasets and protocols*, not the policies.
- *Strongest supporting evidence*: The alphaxiv-surfaced lineage is explicit: "Is Diversity All You Need for Scalable Robotic Manipulation?" ([[2507.06219]]), Data Scaling Laws in Imitation Learning ([[2410.18647]]), Embodiment Scaling Laws in Locomotion ([[2505.05753]]), Neural Scaling Laws in Robotics ([[2405.14005]]), Factored Scaling Curves ([[2505.07728]]). Every one found scaling curves that *bend* and that *which* axis you scale matters more than count — the tactile case is overwhelmingly likely to rhyme: sensing-*principle* coverage will matter more than sensor *count*.
- *The one thing only this perspective says*: Reframe the axis from "number of sensors" to "number of *sensing principles* spanned" — the cross-embodiment wave learned that raw count saturates but *coverage of the underlying physical mode* is what predicts held-out transfer. That single reframe is what separates a citable law from a redundant sweep.

#### Prompt 2 — Contradiction Map

1. **Direct clashes.**
   - *Academic vs Skeptic on whether the bet is a "result"*: Academic says H2 (perception≠policy-SR decoupling) is a clean publishable negative; Skeptic says a monotone-and-saturating H1 curve over 3-4 sensors is a sweep of a near-saturated quantity, not a law. Specific claim in tension: is "retention rises with diversity and clears ≥80% by small N" informative when UniForce already hits 90–120% (all-seen) and FTP-1 +31.6% (unseen, N=21)?
   - *Practitioner vs Card on the held-out unit*: the card treats "sensor" as a fixed held-out entity; Practitioner says within-sensor drift (gel wear, reseat, illumination) moves the distribution as much as a sensor swap, so the N−1 split's "encoder-held-out" cleanliness is partly illusory.
   - *Historian/Economist vs Card on the axis*: card scales sensor *count*; Historian says scale *sensing principles*, Economist says the citable asset is the *protocol*, not the curve — both imply the card's stated headline (a count-based scaling law) is the wrong thing to foreground.

2. **Strongest vs weakest perspective.** Strongest: **Historian** — it is the only one grounding the bet in a directly analogous, already-resolved wave (cross-embodiment scaling), and its reframe (principles, not count) is concrete and de-risking. Weakest: **Economist** — correct but least technically load-bearing; the "ship the protocol" advice is sound but does not change whether the science is true.

3. **The one question that resolves the biggest contradiction.** Does held-out policy-SR retention track the number of distinct *sensing principles* the encoder has seen, or the raw *count* of sensors (which may be near-saturated by N=3-4)? Answering this resolves Academic-vs-Skeptic (is there headroom?) and Historian-vs-Card (which axis?) at once.

4. **What every perspective agrees on (likely true).** No paper in the card or surfaced by alphaxiv runs a strict *encoder-held-out, sweep-over-sensor-count, deployable-policy-SR* retention curve — the specific measurement is genuinely unrun. And policy-SR is not interchangeable with classification/perception accuracy (every perspective treats H2 as real).

5. **The blind spot none addressed.** *Failure mode and safety of the held-out sensor at deployment*: a force-invariant encoder that retains 80% task-SR can still mis-estimate force *direction/magnitude* on an unseen sensor in ways that damage fragile objects or break grasps — none of the five framed retention as a *safety-bounded* quantity (what is the worst-case force error on the held-out sensor, not just mean SR?). Tactile Cross-Training ([[2503.01058]]) reports normal-force MAE <0.92 N precisely because that error, not SR, is what matters for damage-free grasping.

#### Prompt 3 — Synthesis Briefing

**CEO summary.** The E2 bet is well-posed and its central measurement is genuinely unrun: nobody has run a strict encoder-held-out, leave-one-sensor-out sweep over sensor *count* against deployable policy success-rate. But the card oversells the whitespace. The existence and the basic fact of cross-sensor *transfer* are already settled — UniForce transfers zero-shot across vision-based and magnetic sensors, and FTP-1 (confirmed from full text) already reports +31.6% policy-SR on two *unseen* sensors from a 21-sensor corpus. What remains is the *shape of a curve*, and the cross-embodiment-scaling wave that this rhymes with found such curves bend and saturate early, with *which axis* you scale mattering more than count. So this is partially-pre-empted whitespace: a thin, real wedge whose value is mostly in the *protocol and the negative result* (perception≠policy-SR), not in a count-based scaling law over the 3-4 physically-distinct sensing principles that actually exist.

**5 key findings, ranked by reliability.**
1. *The strict encoder-held-out N−1 policy-SR sweep is unrun* (high reliability). Supported by Academic, Skeptic, all (universal agreement); confirmed against FTP-1 full text (two unseen sensors at fixed diversity, not a count sweep) and UniForce note (encoder saw all 3 sensors). Challenged by: nobody on the *fact*, but Skeptic challenges its *informativeness*.
2. *Perception accuracy ≠ deployable policy-SR (H2)* (high reliability, most publishable). Supported by Academic, Practitioner, the card's own table (SITR/T3 report classification, never the curve). Challenged by: none strongly — this is the safest sub-result.
3. *The scaling curve will likely saturate early, with sensing-principle coverage > sensor count* (medium-high). Supported by Historian via five real scaling-law papers (2507.06219, 2410.18647, 2505.05753, 2405.14005, 2505.07728) and Skeptic (only 3-5 distinct principles exist). Challenged by: the card's monotone-rise prediction (H1).
4. *Existence/transfer is settled, narrowing the bet to curve-shape* (medium-high). Supported by Skeptic, Academic; UniForce + FTP-1 + CTTP (18 vs 5 at N=1 source). Challenged by: Academic's caveat that r=−0.74 is partial, not "by construction," grounding.
5. *The defensible contribution is the protocol/leaderboard, not the policy* (medium). Supported by Economist, Historian. Challenged by: Practitioner (a protocol that ignores within-sensor drift is brittle).

**Hidden cross-perspective connection.** Practitioner's within-sensor drift and Historian's "scale principles not count" and the blind-spot's worst-case-force-error are the same underlying point: *the held-out unit is mis-specified*. "Sensor" is neither the right granularity (too coarse: drift moves the distribution within a sensor) nor the right axis (wrong: principle-coverage predicts transfer, not count) nor the right metric (incomplete: mean SR hides damaging force errors). Fixing the unit of analysis fixes three perspectives at once.

**Actionable insight — DE-RISK, do not commit blind.** The bet is real but thin and partially pre-empted; commit *only* to the reframed, protocol-first version. Concretely: (1) Reframe the x-axis from sensor *count* to *sensing-principle coverage* (optical / magnetic / capacitive / piezoresistive / array). (2) Lead with H2 as a standalone negative result (perception-accuracy is not a policy-SR proxy) — it is publishable even if H1 saturates. (3) Add a worst-case held-out *force-error* report alongside mean SR (close the safety blind spot). (4) Ship the leave-one-sensing-principle-out split as a track on roto 2.0 + ManiFeel — the citable asset. *Concrete next experiment / go-no-go*: take UniForce's force-equilibrium encoder, train on principles {optical, magnetic}, hold out {capacitive *and* piezoresistive} *from the encoder*, measure deployable policy-SR retention on ManiFeel's encoder-swap harness AND held-out classification accuracy, and plot SR-retention vs classification on the same axes. If they decouple (H2 holds) you have a paper regardless of H1; if held-out policy-SR retention is already ≥80% at this minimal 2-principle setup, the count-scaling law has no headroom and you pivot to the safety/force-error angle. Pre-register, before running, the retention delta across principle-count that would count as a "diversity gain."

**Frontier question that would change everything.** Is there a *minimal physical basis* of contact (e.g., the full contact wrench plus local geometry) such that any tactile sensor is a lossy projection of it — making cross-sensor invariance a reconstruction problem with a provable information floor, rather than a data-scaling problem? If so, the ceiling is computable a priori from each sensor's measurement basis, and the entire N−1 sweep is replaced by a per-sensor information-loss bound — turning E2's empirical scaling law into a theorem.

---

#### Prompt 4 — Peer Review (independent)

*Reviewer stance: skeptical Stanford robotics faculty. I read the E2 card (Manipulation.md L1232–1318), the analyst part (019-E2-1), and spot-checked the load-bearing numbers against the KH notes for UniForce (2602.01153), FTP-1 (2606.13102), TaF-VLA (2601.20321), and the five "scaling-law" papers Historian leans on.*

**Verification done before grading.**
- UniForce numbers check out in KH: z5↔Fz **r=−0.74±0.024, R²≈55.2%**, wiping-SR **20%→50–60% single-sensor / 80% bilateral on one task**, spans vision-based + magnetic. The briefing's use is faithful, and Academic's "partial grounding, not by-construction" caveat is *correct*: 55% of variance on one latent dim is a correlation, not a proof of invariance.
- FTP-1 **+31.6% on unseen sensors / +17.2% seen** confirmed in KH. **But** the briefing's specifics — "two unseen sensors," "21-sensor corpus," "Xense/Contactile," "3,000-hr corpus" — are **not in the KH note**; they come from the analyst's "FTP-1 full text" reading and are unverified here. The whole "it's a single point, not a curve" argument rests on the "two unseen sensors at fixed diversity" claim, which I could not confirm from the vault. This is the soft spot under finding #1's most confident sub-claim.
- Historian's "five real scaling-law papers" (finding #3): only **2 of 5 are in KH** (2410.18647, 2505.05753). Three (2507.06219, 2405.14005, 2505.07728) are **MISSING from the vault**, and **none of the five is cited in the E2 card**. Calling these "five real scaling-law papers" oversells the evidence base for the single most consequential strategic claim (saturate-early / coverage>count).
- TaF-VLA "60.3% family-level **ceiling**": the KH note frames 60.3% as *zero-shot generalization to unseen sensors* plus a plug-and-play +6.7–33.3% lift on ACT/DP — i.e. a **positive transfer result**, not a "ceiling." Both the card and the briefing repeatedly invoke it as a ceiling; that is a tendentious reframe of a paper that actually supports transfer working.

---

**1. CONFIDENCE SCORES (1–10).**

1. *The strict encoder-held-out N−1 policy-SR sweep is unrun* — **8/10.** The strongest claim, with universal agreement and no counterexample in 28 cited rows. Docked two points because the supporting contrast ("FTP-1 = single point, two unseen, fixed diversity") relies on full-text specifics not reproducible from the vault; the *fact* of unrun-ness is safe, the *characterization of how close FTP-1 comes* is not.

2. *Perception accuracy ≠ deployable policy-SR (H2)* — **7/10.** Conceptually the safest and most publishable result, well-motivated by the metric-substitution pattern in the card's own table (SITR/T3 report classification). Held below 8 because it is asserted, not yet demonstrated: no cited paper actually shows the decouple (classification high, policy-SR low) on the *same* held-out sensor — it is a plausible hypothesis the briefing treats as near-established.

3. *Curve saturates early; sensing-principle coverage > count* — **5/10.** The most strategically load-bearing claim and the least-supported. The historical analogy (cross-embodiment scaling) is real and reasonable, but 3 of the 5 anchor papers are absent from the vault and none is cited in the card, so the "five real papers" framing inflates confidence. It is a defensible prior, not an evidenced finding.

4. *Existence/transfer is settled, narrowing the bet to curve-shape* — **6/10.** UniForce + FTP-1 + CTTP do show transfer *works*, but "settled" is too strong: UniForce is one task / one sensor-pair-type, FTP-1's diversity is unverified here, TaF-VLA is mischaracterized as a ceiling. Transfer is *demonstrated in narrow regimes*, not *settled*. Academic's own caveat (r=−0.74 is partial) undercuts the "settled" verb.

5. *The defensible contribution is the protocol/leaderboard, not the policy* — **6/10.** Sound strategic judgment (benchmark-creation is high-leverage pre-standardization), and Practitioner's within-sensor-drift objection is a real threat to the protocol's validity. Medium because it is an incentive argument, not a scientific finding, and it quietly assumes the protocol can be shipped before the next omnibus benchmark absorbs it — an assumption the Economist *raised* and then waved away.

**Mean = 6.4. Lowest = 5 (finding #3).**

---

**2. WEAKEST LINK.** Finding #3 (saturate-early, coverage>count) is the weakest *and* the most consequential, because the entire "DE-RISK, reframe the x-axis" recommendation hangs on it. To verify, I need: (a) the three missing scaling-law papers (2507.06219, 2405.14005, 2505.07728) actually read and confirmed to report *saturating* curves where *axis choice* beat *count* — not just titles that sound supportive; and (b) at least one *tactile-specific* data point that principle-coverage predicts cross-sensor transfer better than raw count. Right now the claim is a transplant from a different modality (locomotion/manipulation embodiment) onto touch, with no touch evidence that the analogy holds. Runner-up weak link: the FTP-1 "two unseen sensors at fixed diversity" claim, unverifiable from the vault.

---

**3. BIAS CHECK.** **Historian over-dominated.** The synthesis's spine — "this is the cross-embodiment wave again, so it'll saturate, so reframe count→principles" — is entirely Historian's reframe, and it is promoted to the headline CEO verdict ("partially-pre-empted whitespace") and the #1 actionable. The analyst even named Historian the "strongest perspective." The problem: Historian's evidence is the thinnest (3/5 papers missing), yet it shapes the conclusion most. Academic is the second strongest contributor but its most important caveat (transfer is *partial*, not settled) was softened into a parenthetical. Practitioner's within-sensor-drift point is excellent and got folded into the "hidden connection" but never priced into the go/no-go. Economist and Skeptic are present but subordinate. Net: a **Historian-anchored, analogy-driven** synthesis where the analogy is asserted more confidently than the vault supports.

---

**4. MISSING PERSPECTIVE — the Metrologist / Sensor Physicist.** No perspective owns the *physics of the measurement basis*. The briefing's own "frontier question" (is there a minimal contact wrench such that each sensor is a lossy projection, giving a computable information floor?) is exactly this missing angle — and it would *change the conclusions*, not just decorate them. If the information floor is partly computable a priori, then: (i) the "fundamental floor vs data-limited" falsifier becomes *decidable* rather than the heads-I-win framing Skeptic flagged; (ii) "sensing-principle coverage" stops being a hand-picked 5-bin axis (optical/magnetic/capacitive/piezoresistive/array) and becomes a *measured* span of the wrench-plus-geometry basis; and (iii) the entire N−1 empirical sweep could be partially *replaced* by per-sensor loss bounds. A sensor-physics reviewer would also kill the unexamined assumption that "force" is the right invariant at all — optical sensors measure *deformation field / local geometry*, and reducing everything to a 3- or 6-axis force vector (UniForce's choice) may discard the contact-geometry signal that CTTP and UniTac-NV show is what actually transfers. That single objection threatens H1 and the whole force-grounding thesis.

---

**5. OVERALL GRADE — B / B−.** This is a genuinely good piece of adversarial strategy work: it correctly identifies that the card oversells whitespace, it surfaces the real wedge (H2 negative result + protocol), and the go/no-go experiment is concrete and pre-registerable. The five-perspective structure produced non-trivial tension (Academic-vs-Skeptic on "is a curve a result"), and the "fix the unit of analysis" synthesis is a real insight. It loses a full grade for **evidence hygiene**: the strategic headline (saturate-early) rests on a paper set that is 60% absent from the vault and 0% cited in the card, "settled" overclaims transfer that the same briefing elsewhere calls partial, and TaF-VLA's positive result is bent into a "ceiling." A Stanford committee would not fail this, but would send it back to separate *what the vault demonstrates* from *what the cross-modality analogy assumes*.

**Top fixes (ranked).**
1. **Stop calling the saturate-early claim "five real papers."** Either read and cite the three missing scaling-law papers in the vault, or downgrade finding #3 from "medium-high" to "prior/analogy" and label it explicitly as untested-on-touch. This is the single most important fix.
2. **Verify or retract the FTP-1 "two unseen sensors / 21-sensor corpus" specifics** — the "it's a point not a curve" argument (and thus the "partially pre-empted" verdict) depends on numbers not in the KH note.
3. **Soften "settled" → "demonstrated in narrow regimes,"** and correct the TaF-VLA "ceiling" mischaracterization (KH frames 60.3% as a positive zero-shot + plug-and-play lift).
4. **Promote the Metrologist/sensor-physics angle from a "frontier question" footnote into the go/no-go**: before scaling sensor count, test whether *force* or *contact-geometry* is the load-bearing invariant — because if it's geometry, UniForce's force-grounding caps H1 independent of N.
5. **Price Practitioner's within-sensor drift into the protocol design**, not just the "hidden connection": a leave-one-sensor-out split that ignores intra-sensor distribution shift will overstate portability and is the first thing a deploying reviewer attacks.
